        \item Every $\Phi'$-exact distributor with $\Phi'$-cocomplete domain is $\Phi$-exact.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

This relation defines a preorder on the collection of classes of weights, and the most practical are the maximal elements with respect to inclusion:

\begin{definition}
    \index{class of weights!colimit saturation}
    Let $\Phi$ be a class of weights, the \textbf{colimit saturation} $\Phi^{\colim}$ of $\Phi$ is the largest class such that $\Phi^{\colim} \colimeq \Phi$. It is \textbf{colimit saturated} is $\Phi = \Phi^{\colim}$.
\end{definition}

We establish of few properties that will be used when linking cocompletions and presheaf objects.

\begin{lemma}
    $(-)^{\colim}$ defines a closure operator whose fixed points are the colimit saturated class of weights. For a class of weights $\Phi$, we have the following:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\Phi^{\colim}$ is replete.
        \item $B(1, f)\in\Phi^{\colim}$ for each arrow $f : A \to B$.
        \item Given two compatible weights $\vec p, \vec q \in\Phi^{\colim}, (p_1, \cdots, p_m, q_1, \cdots, q_n)\in\Phi^{\colim}$.
        \item If $\vec p\in\Phi^{\colim}$ and $p_1\odot_L\cdots\odot_L p_i$ exists (for $1\leq i\leq m$) then $(p_1\odot_L \cdots\odot_L p_i, p_{i+1}, \cdots, p_m)\in\Phi^{\colim}$.
        \item If $\vec p\in\Phi^{\colim}$ and $p_i\odot\cdots\odot p_j$ exists (for $1\leq i\leq j\leq m$) then $(p_1, \cdots, p_{i-1}, p_i\odot \cdots\odot p_j, p_{j+1}, \cdots, p_m)\in\Phi^{\colim}$.
        \item If $p\in\Phi^{\colim}, p(1, f)\in\Phi^{\colim}$
    \end{enumerate}
    Finally, $\Phi\colimeq\Phi'$ if and only if $\Phi^{\colim} \colimeq\Phi'^{\colim}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    This is a series of easy check:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Repletion comes from essential uniqueness of colimits.
        \item For every arrow $g : B \to C$, its $B(1, f)$-colimit is $g\circ f$.
        \item Follows from \ref{lem:ext_lift:stab_split}.
        \item Follows from \ref{lem:ext_lift:stab_wcomp}.
        \item Follows from \ref{lem:ext_lift:stab_comp}.
        \item Follows from $p(1, f) = p\odot B(1, f)$ and the previous items; or \ref{lem:co_lim:res}.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

The definition of a $\Phi$-cocompletion might look unusually strong because of the axiom regarding distributors. This is coherent with the rest of the definition internal to a virtual equipment, where the condition on distributors eliminate pathologies.

\begin{definition}
    \index{$\Phi$-cocompletion}
    \label{def:cocomp}
    Let $\Phi$ be a class of weights and $A$ an object. A \textbf{$\Phi$-cocompletion} of $A$ consists of an object $\coc\Phi A$ and an arrow $\coce\Phi A \to \coc\Phi A$ such that:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\coc\Phi A$ is $\Phi$-cocomplete.
        \item For each arrow $f : A \to X$ with $\Phi$-cocomplete codomain, the left extension $\coce\Phi A \plext f : \coc\Phi A \to X$ exists, the unit $f \To (\coce\Phi A \plext f) \circ \coce\Phi A$ is invertible and $\coce\Phi A\plext f$ is the essentially unique $\Phi$-cocontinuous arrow $\widetilde{f} : \coc\Phi A\to X$ such that $f\simeqq \widetilde{f} \circ \coce\Phi A$.
        \item For each distributor $p : A \dfrom X$, the right lift $p\rift\coc\Phi A (\coce\Phi A, 1) : \coc\Phi A \dfrom X$ exists, the cell $(p\rift\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1))(\coce\Phi A, 1) \To p$ is invertible and $p\rift\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1)$ is the essentially unique $\Phi$-exact distributor $\widetilde{p} : \coc\Phi A\dfrom X$ such that $p\simeqq \widetilde{p}(\coce\Phi A, 1)$.
    \end{enumerate}
    An object \textbf{$\Phi$-cocompletable} if it admits a $\Phi$-cocompletion.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    Consequently, the conjoint of $\coce\Phi A$ is always lifting. Plus, since an arrow is $\Phi$-cocontinuous if and only if its conjoint is $\Phi$-exact, the condition of $\Phi$-cocontinuous arrows can be replaced with
    \begin{itemize}
        \item If $p : A \dfrom X$ is corepresentable and $X$ is $\Phi$-cocomplete, then $p\rift\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1)$ is corepresentable.
    \end{itemize}
    The notion of cocompletion is compatible with the earlier discussion on colimit equivalence: for $\Phi$ and $\Phi'$ be classes of weights such that $\Phi\colimeq\Phi'$, an arrow $j : A \to E$ exhibits the $\Phi$-cocompletion of $A$ if and only if it exhibits the $\Phi'$-cocompletion of $A$.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:cocomp_ff}
    Every $\Phi$-cocompletion $\coce\Phi A : A \to \coc\Phi A$ is fully faithful.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    By definition there are isomorphisms
    $$p\rift\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, \coce\Phi A) \simeqq (p\rift\coc\phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1))(\coce\Phi A, 1) \simeqq p \simeqq p\rift A(1, 1)$$
    Hence a natural bijection between cells $A(1, 1)\To p$ and $\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, \coce\Phi A) \To p$, implying $A(1, 1)\simeqq\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, \coce\Phi A)$, so $\coce\Phi A$ is fully faithful.
\end{proof}

This lemma can fail without the axiom on distributors, justifying its existence.

\begin{corollary}
    Let $\Phi$ be a class of weights and $A$ a $\Phi$-cocompletable object. Then $\widetilde{A(1, 1)}\simeqq\coc\Phi A(1, \coce\Phi A)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    $\coc\Phi A(1, \coce\Phi A)(\coce\Phi A, 1) \simeqq \coc\Phi A(1, 1)$ by Lemma \ref{lem:cocomp_ff}, and we conclude by essential uniqueness.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:cocomp_leftext_abs}
    For $\coce\Phi A : A \to \coc\Phi A$ a $\Phi$-cocompletion, left extensions along $\coce\Phi A$ and $\Phi$-colimits in $\coc\Phi A$ are $\coce\Phi A$-absolute.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Every rift lift through $\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1)$ is $\Phi$-exact, and we conclude by \ref{lem:lifting_abs_iff_lift_respect}.
\end{proof}

The following proposition gives a few criteria to check $\Phi$-cocontinuity. Note that in classical category theory, $(a)\iff(b)$ is immediate.

\begin{proposition}
    Let $\coce\Phi A : A \to \coc\Phi A$ a $\Phi$-cocompletion. For $f : \coc\Phi A \to X$, the following are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $f$ is $\Phi$-cocontinuous.
        \item $f$ has rank $\coce\Phi A$
        \item $f$ is a pointwise left extension along $\coce\Phi A$.
        \item $f$ preserves $\coce\Phi A$-absolute colimits.
    \end{enumerate}
    For $p : \coc\Phi A\dfrom X$, the following are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $p$ is $\Phi$-exact.
        \item $p$ has rank $\coce\Phi A$.
        \item $p$ is a right lift through $\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1)$.
        \item $p$ respects $\coce\Phi A$-absolute colimits.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    $\coce\Phi A$ is fully faithful by Lemma \ref{lem:cocomp_ff}, so Proposition \ref{prop:rank_conditions} gives $(b) \iff (c) \implies (d)$. $(d) \implies (a)$ is given by Lemma \ref{lem:cocomp_leftext_abs} since all $\Phi$-colimits in $\coc\Phi A$ are $\coce\Phi A$-absolute.

    For $p$ a $\Phi$-exact distributor, the right lift $p(\coce\Phi A, 1)\rift\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1)$ is the unique $\Phi$-cocontinuous $\widetilde{p(\coce\Phi A, 1)}$ such that $p(\coce\Phi A, 1) \simeqq \widetilde{p(\coce\Phi A, 1)}(\coce\Phi A, 1)$ exhibiting $p$ as a right lift through $\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1)$, therefore $(a) \implies (c)$. For an arrow, take $p = X(f, 1)$ which exhibits $f$ as the pointwise left extension $\coce\Phi A \plext (f\coce\Phi A)$.
\end{proof}

This corollary is a sanity check: the object essentially describes its cocompletion.

\begin{corollary}
    \label{cor:cocomp_dense}
    Every $\Phi$-cocompletion $\coce\Phi A : A \to \coc\Phi A$ is dense.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    $\id_{\coc\Phi A}$ is $\Phi$-cocontinuous, hence has rank $\coce\Phi A$, hence $\coce\Phi A \plext \coce\Phi A \simeqq \id_{\coc\Phi A}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lem:cocomp_colimsat}
    Let $\coce\Phi A : A \to \coc\Phi A$ a $\Phi$-cocompletion. Then $\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1)$ is in the colimit saturation of $\Phi$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    $\Phi$-cocomplete objects admit left extensions along $\coce\Phi A$, $\Phi$-cocontinuous arrows preserve and $\Phi$-exact respect $\coce\Phi A$-absolute colimits, including left extensions along $\coce\Phi A$ by Lemma \ref{lem:cocomp_leftext_abs}.
\end{proof}

We deduce an alternative characterisation of cocompletions which we will use in Subsection \ref{subsec:psh_cocomp} to establish the link between presheaf objects and cocompletions.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{th:cocomp_chararct}
    An arrow $j : A \to E$ exhibits the $\Phi$-cocompletion of $A$ if and only if
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $E$ is $\Phi$-complete.
        \item $E(j, 1)$ is in the colimit saturation of $\Phi$.
        \item $E(j, 1)$ is lifting and right lifts through $E(j, 1)$ are $\Phi$-exact.
        \item $j$ is dense and fully faithful.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    First assume that $j$ exhibits the $\Phi$-cocompletion of $A$, then (a) and (c) are by definition, (b) by Lemma \ref{lem:cocomp_colimsat} and (d) by Corollary \ref{cor:cocomp_dense} and Lemma \ref{lem:cocomp_ff}.

    Supposing (a) through (d) hold, we verify the conditions in Definition \ref{def:cocomp}. (a) is by assumption (a). For (b), since $E(j, 1)$ is in the colimit saturation of $\Phi$, the left extension $j \plext f : E \to X$ where $f$ is an arrow with $\Phi$-cocomplete codomain, exists and corepresents $X(f, 1)\rift E(j, 1)$. Finally for (c), given $p : A \dfrom X$, existence and $\Phi$-exactness of the right lifts exist by assumption (c) and the cell $(p\rift E(j, 1))(j, 1)\To p$ is invertible by full faithfulness of $j$ using Lemma \ref{lem:ff_res}. For $\widetilde p : E \dfrom E$ such that $p \simeqq \widetilde{p}(j, 1)$, and using density of $j$ (assumption (d)),  $\Phi$-exactness and $E(j, 1)$ being in the colimit saturation of $\Phi$, we have:
    $$\widetilde p \simeqq \widetilde{p}(j\plext j, 1) \simeqq \widetilde{p}(j, 1) \rift E(j, 1) \simeqq p\rift E(j, 1)$$
    Hence essential uniqueness.
\end{proof}

It is possible to get another characterisation of those objects that are equivalent to a $\Phi$-cocompletion with a useful criterion on adjointness.

\begin{proposition}
    Let $j : A \to E$ a dense arrow with $\Phi$-cocomplete codomain. An arrow $f : E \to X$ admits a right adjoint if and only if $(fj)\plext j$ exists.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Since $j$ is dense, and by Lemma \ref{lem:pt_ext_props}, $(fj)\plext j \simeqq f \plext (j \plext j) \simeqq f \plext 1$. Being a $\Phi$-colimit, it is preserved by $f$ and we conclude by Corollary \ref{cor:adjoint_arrow_theorem}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    \label{cor:cocomp_adj_iff_lext}
    Let $f : A \to X$ an arrow with $\Phi$-cocompletable domain and $\Phi$-cocomplete codomain. Then $\widetilde f : \coc\Phi A \to X$ admits a right adjoint if and only if $f\plext 1$ exists.
\end{corollary}

We start with a simple recognition principle for $\Phi$-cocompletion:

\begin{proposition}
    Let $A$ a $\Phi$-cocompletable object, $E$ an object. The following are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $E\simeq\coc\Phi A$.
        \item There exists a dense and fully faithful $\Phi$-atom $j : A \to E$ with $\Phi$-cocomplete codomain for which $j \plext \coce\Phi A$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Take $j$ to be $\coce\Phi A$ which satisfies the hypotheses for Lemmas \ref{lem:cocomp_ff}, \ref{lem:cocomp_leftext_abs} and Corollary \ref{cor:cocomp_dense}. Conversely, by Corollary \ref{cor:cocomp_adj_iff_lext}, $\coce\Phi A\plext j\dashv j\plext\coce\Phi A$. Since the conditions of Lemma \ref{lem:left_witness_equiv} are satisfied, and $j$ is a $\Phi$-atom, $\coce\Phi A\plext j$ is $j$-absolute.
\end{proof}

And, more generally, we get a range a tests for $\Phi$-cocompleteness...

\begin{theorem}
    \label{th:cocomp_tests}
    The following are equivalent for an object $A$
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $A$ is $\Phi$-cocomplete.
        \item $A$ is reflective in a $\Phi$-cocomplete object.
    \end{enumerate}
    And if $A$ admits a $\Phi$-cocompletion $\coce\Phi A : A \to \coc\Phi A$, the following are also equivalent
    \begin{enumerate}[resume]
        \item For every object $X$ with a $\Phi$-cocompletion $\coce\Phi X$ and each $g : X \to A$, the left extension $\coce\Phi X \plext g : X \to \coc\Phi X$.
        \item The left extension $\coce\Phi A \plext 1_A : \coc\Phi A \to C$ exists.
        \item For every weight $\vec p\in\Phi^{\colim}$, $A$ admits the colimit $\colim^{\vec p}A_1$.
        \item $\coce\Phi A : A \to \coc\Phi A$ admits a left adjoint.
        \item $A$ is reflective in a $\Phi$-cocompletion.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    $(a)\implies (b)\land(c)\land(e)$, $(c)\implies(d)$ and $(f)\implies(g)\implies(b)$ are immediate. $(b)\implies(a)$ by Lemma \ref{lem:refl_adj_colims}, $(e)\implies(d)$ by Lemma \ref{lem:cocomp_colimsat}, and $(d)\implies(e)$ because $\coce\Phi A\plext 1_A$ is the left adjoint.
    \begin{align*}
        A(\coce\Phi A\plext 1_A, 1) &\simeqq A(1, 1)\rift \coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1) & (\coce\Phi A\plext 1_A)\\
                                    &\simeqq \coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi 1, \coce\Phi A)\rift\coc\Phi A(\coce\Phi A, 1) & (\ref{lem:cocomp_ff})\\
                                    &\simeqq \coc\Phi A(1, \coce\Phi A) & (\ref{cor:cocomp_dense})
    \end{align*}
\end{proof}

...And the corresponding characterisation for $\Phi$-cocontinuity:

\begin{corollary}
    Let $A$ and $B$ $\Phi$-cocompletable objects. The following are equivalent for an arrow $f : A \to B$:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $f$ is $\Phi$-cocontinuous.
        \item Given a pseudocommutative square of arrow on the left where the vertical arrows exhibit reflective inclusions, the mate on the right is invertible.
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                B \ar[r, "b", ""{anchor=center,name=t}] & B'\\
                \ar[u, hook, "r"] A \ar[r, "a"', ""{anchor=center,name=b}] & A' \ar[u, hook, "r'"']
                \ar[from=t, to=b, phantom, "\simeqq"{anchor=center}]
            \end{tikzcd}
            \hspace{5em}
            \begin{tikzcd}
                \ar[d, "\ell"'] B \ar[r, "b"] & \ar[dl, Rightarrow] B' \ar[d, "\ell'"]\\
                A \ar[r, "a"'] & A'
            \end{tikzcd}\]
    \end{enumerate}
    If $A$ admits a $\Phi$-cocompletion $\coce\Phi A : A \to \coc\Phi A$ then the following are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}[resume]
        \item For every object $X$ admitting a $\Phi$-cocompletion $\coce\Phi X : X \to \coc\Phi X$ and each $g : X \to A$, the left extension $\coce\Phi X \plext g : \coc\Phi X\to A$ is preserved by $f$.
        \item The left extension $\coce\Phi A\plext 1_A$ is preserved by $f$.
        \item For every weight $\vec p\in\Phi^{\colim}$, the weighted colimit $\colim^{\vec p}1_A$ is preserved by $f$.
    \end{enumerate}
    Furthermore, if $B$ admits a $\Phi$-cocompletion $\coce\Phi B : B \to \coc\Phi B$ then the following are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}[resume]
        \item As in (b), but $r = \coce\Phi A$, $r' = \coce\Phi B$ and $b = \coc\Phi f := \coce\Phi A\plext (\coce\Phi B\circ a)$.
        \item As in (b), but $B$ and $B'$ are $\Phi$-cocompletion and $b\simeqq r\plext(r'\circ a)$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    The proof is the similar as in Theorem \ref{th:cocomp_tests}. The hardest is $(d)\implies(f)$, we have
    $$(\coce\Phi A\plext1_B)\circ(\coce\Phi A\plext(\coce\Phi B\circ a)) \simeqq \coce\Phi A\plext ((\coce\Phi B\plext 1_B)\circ\coce\Phi B\circ a)\simeqq \coce\Phi A\plext a$$
    Thus the cell witnessing preservation of $\coce\Phi A\plext 1_A$ is the mate.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Presheaf objects as cocompletions}
\label{subsec:psh_cocomp}

As mentioned before, classically the presheaf construction also gives the free cocompletion for a small category. The following theorem gives sufficient conditions for presheaf object to be a cocompletion.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{th:psh_cocomp}
    Let $\Phi$ be a class of weights, $\PP$ a class of distributors and $A$ a $\PP$-admissible object. Suppose that
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item For every weight $\vec p\in\Phi$ and distributor $q\in\PP$, the left composite $q\odot_L\vec p$ and is in $\PP^\simeqq$.
        \item $\pi_A^\PP$ is in $\Phi^{\colim}$.
        \item $\pi_A^\PP : A \dfrom \psh\PP A$ is lifting.
    \end{enumerate}
    Then $\yo_A^\PP : A \to \psh\PP A$ exhibits the $\Phi$-cocompletion of $A$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    We use Theorem \ref{th:cocomp_chararct}:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\psh\PP A$ is $\Phi$-cocomplete by Proposition \ref{prop:psh_colims}.
        \item $\psh\PP A(\yo_A^\PP, 1)$ is in the colimit saturation since it contains $\pi_A^\PP$ and is replete.
        \item $\pi_A^\PP$ is lifting and right lifts through $\pi_A^\PP$ are $\Phi$-exact by Lemma \ref{lem:lifting_abs_iff_lift_respect} where $\pi_A^\PP$-absoluteness of $\Phi$-colimits is obtained with Proposition \ref{prop:psh_colims}.
        \item $\yo_A^\PP$ is dense and fully faithful by Proposition \ref{prop:monic_dense_atom_equiv}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

Restricting to a class of unary weights, we get:

\begin{corollary}
    Let $\PP$ be a class of distributors closed under left-composites and $A$ a $\PP$-admissible object. Then $\psh\PP A$ is $\PP$-cocomplete and every $\PP$-colimit in $\psh\PP A$ is $\PP$-absolute. If $\pi_A^\PP : A \dfrom \psh\PP A$ is lifting, then $\yo_A^\PP$ exhibits the $\PP$-cocompletion of $A$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    Apply respectively Proposition \ref{prop:psh_colims} and Theorem \ref{th:psh_cocomp}.
\end{proof}


\section{Instantiations}
\label{sec:instances}

In this last section, we construct multiple virtual equipments associated to different flavours of category theories. By instantiating certain results, we check how well formal category theory in virtual equipments can capture the appropriate notions and recover the usual theorems.

\subsection{Enriched categories}
\label{subsec:enriched}

Many constructions rely on the existence of additional structure of the homsets of categories: additive or linear categories, dg-categories, 2-categories... It is possible to axiomatize this approach: we \textit{enrich} a category. Although it is possible, as was done in the article \cite{arkor2026presheavescocompletionsformalcategory}, to enrich a category in a virtual bicategory (or even a virtual double category), we choose to stick to monoidal categories (as was done in \cite{arkor2024formaltheoryrelativemonads}) as it is more common although less general.

Most of the theory of enriched category theory can be found in \cite{kelly}.

\begin{definition}
    \index{category!monoidal}
    A \textbf{monoidal category} $(\cat{V}, \otimes, I, a, l, r)$, usually only written $\cat{V}$ comprises of the following data
    \begin{itemize}
        \item A category $\cat{V}$.
        \item A functor $\otimes:\cat{V}\times\cat{V} \to \cat{V}$.
        \item An object $I$ of $\cat{V}$, the \textbf{unit}.
        \item Three natural isomorphisms $a, l, r$ with signatures $a_{XYZ} : (X \otimes Y) \otimes Z \to X \otimes (Y \otimes Z)$, $l_X : I \otimes X \to X$ and $r_X : X \otimes I \to I$ for $X, Y, Z$ objects in $\cat{V}$; called respectively the associator and the left and right unitors.
    \end{itemize}
    Such that the following diagrams commute
    \[\begin{tikzcd}[sep=scriptsize]
        {(X \otimes I) \otimes Y} \ar[rr, "a_{XIY}"]
        \ar[rdd, "r \otimes \id_Y"'] && {X \otimes (I \otimes Y)}
        \ar[ldd, "\id_X \otimes l"]\\
        \\
        & {X \otimes Y}
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    \[\begin{tikzcd}[sep=scriptsize]
        & {((W \otimes X) \otimes Y) \otimes Z} & \\
        \\
        {(W \otimes (X \otimes Y)) \otimes Z} && {(W \otimes X) \otimes (Y \otimes Z)} \\
        \\
        {W \otimes ((X \otimes Y) \otimes Z)} && {W \otimes (X \otimes (Y \otimes Z))}
        \arrow["{a_{WXY} \otimes \id_Z}", from=1-2, to=3-1]
        \arrow["{a_{(W \otimes X)YZ}}"', from=1-2, to=3-3]
        \arrow["{a_{W(X \otimes Y)Z}}", from=3-1, to=5-1]
        \arrow["{a_{WX(Y \otimes Z)}}"', from=3-3, to=5-3]
        \arrow["{\id_W \otimes a_{XYZ}}", from=5-1, to=5-3]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
\end{definition}

Given two monoidal categories $(\cat V_1, \otimes_1, I_1$ and $(\cat V_2, \otimes_2, I_2)$, it is natural to ask for a notion of morphism:

\begin{definition}
    \index{functor!lax monoidal}
    Let $(\cat V_1, \otimes_1, I_1$ and $(\cat V_2, \otimes_2, I_2)$ be two monoidal categories. A \textbf{lax monoidal functor} from $\cat V_1$ to $\cat V_2$ comprises of the following data:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item A functor $F : \cat V_1 \to \cat V_2$
        \item A morphism $\eta : 1_{\cat V_1} \to F1_{\cat V_2}$
        \item A natural transformation $\mu_{x, y} : Fx\otimes_2 Fy \to F(x\otimes_1y)$
    \end{itemize}
    Satisfying associativity and unitality with respect to the left and right unitors of each category.
\end{definition}

Having defined a first good class of bases of enrichment, it is now possible to enrich category there.

\begin{definition}
    \index{category!$\cat{V}$-enriched}
    A \textbf{$\cat{V}$-category} $\cat{C}$ (or \textbf{$\cat{V}$-enriched category}) comprises of the following data
    \begin{itemize}
        \item A class $\ob\cat{C}$, the \textit{objects} of $\cat{C}$.
        \item An object $\cat{C}(A, B)$ of $\cat{V}$ for $A, B$ objects of $\cat{C}$.
        \item A morphism $M : \cat{C}(B, C) \otimes \cat{C}(A, B)$, the \textit{composition law}.
        \item An \textit{identity} morphism $i_A : I \to \cat{C}(A, A)$.
    \end{itemize}
    Such that the associativity and unitality diagrams are commutative:
    \[\begin{tikzcd}[sep=scriptsize]
        {(\cat{C}(C, D) \otimes \cat{C}(B, C)) \otimes \cat{C}(A, B)} && {\cat{C}(C, D) \otimes (\cat{C}(B, C) \otimes \cat{C}(A, B))} \\
        \\
        {\cat{C}(B, D)\otimes \cat{C}(A, B)} && {\cat{C}(C, D) \otimes \cat{C}(A, C)} \\
        \\
        & {\cat{C}(A, D)}
        \arrow["a", from=1-1, to=1-3]
        \arrow["{M\otimes \id}", from=1-1, to=3-1]
        \arrow["{\id \otimes M}", from=1-3, to=3-3]
        \arrow["M"', from=3-1, to=5-2]
        \arrow["M", from=3-3, to=5-2]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    \[\begin{tikzcd}[sep=scriptsize]
        {\cat{C}(B, B) \otimes \cat{C}(A, B)} && {\cat{C}(A, B)} && {\cat{C}(A, B) \otimes \cat{C}(A, A)} \\
        \\
        {I \otimes \cat{C}(A, B)} &&&& {\cat{C}(A, B) \otimes I}
        \arrow["M", from=1-1, to=1-3]
        \arrow["M"', from=1-5, to=1-3]
        \arrow["{i_B \otimes \id}", from=3-1, to=1-1]
        \arrow["l"', from=3-1, to=1-3]
        \arrow["r", from=3-5, to=1-3]
        \arrow["{\id \otimes i_A}"', from=3-5, to=1-5]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    Common examples include: $\cat{Set}$-enriched categories (known as locally small categories), $\cat{Ab}$-enriched categories (known as additive categories), $\cat{Vect}_k$ (known as $k$-linear categories), $\cat{Ch}_k$-enriched categories (known as dg-categories) and $\cat{Cat}$-enriched categories (known as 2-categories). More exotic examples include $\bb 2$-enriched categories (posets) and $\overline{\bb{R}^+}$-enriched categories (metric ($\otimes := +$) or ultrametric ($\otimes := \max$) spaces).
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
    \index{$\cat{V}$-enriched!category}\index{$\cat{V}$-enriched!natural transformation}
    A \textbf{$\cat{V}$-functor} $F : \cat{A} \to \cat{B}$ comprises of a function $F : \ob\cat{A} \to \ob\cat{B}$ and for all $A, B$ objects of $\cat{A}$, a morphism $F_{AB} : \cat{A}(A, B) \to \cat{B}(FA, FB)$ de $\cat{V}$ such that $F$ is compatible with composition and units:
    \[\begin{tikzcd}[sep=small]
        \cat{A}(B, C) \otimes \cat{A}(A, B) \ar[rr, "M"] \ar[dd, "F \otimes F"']
            && \cat{A}(A, C) \ar[dd, "F"]\\
        \\
        \cat{B}(FB, FC) \otimes \cat{B}(FA, FB) \ar[rr, "M"] && \cat{B}(FA, FC)
    \end{tikzcd}
    \begin{tikzcd}[sep=small]
        && \cat{A}(A, A) \ar[dddd, "F"]\\
        \\
        I \ar[uurr, "i_A"] \ar[ddrr, "i_{FA}"]\\
        \\
        && \cat{B}(FA, FA)
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    A \textbf{$\cat{V}$-natural transformation} $\alpha : F \to G$ comprises of morphisms $\alpha_A : I \to \cat{B}(FA, GA)$ such that:
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        & I \otimes \cat{A}(A, B) \ar[r, "\alpha_B\otimes F"] & \cat{B}(FB, GB) \otimes \cat{B}(FA, FB) \ar[rd, start anchor=east, "M"]\\
        \cat{A}(A, B) \ar[ru, "l^{-1}"] \ar[rd, "r^{-1}"] &&& \cat{B}(FA, GB)\\
        & \cat{A}(A, B) \otimes I \ar[r, "G \otimes \alpha_A"] & \cat{B}(GA, GB) \otimes \cat{B}(FA, GA) \ar[ru, start anchor=east, "M"]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
\end{definition}

It is common to add hypothesis on the enriching category: for example, asking for $\otimes$ to be symmetric, asking for $(-\otimes X)$ to have a right adjoint (viewed as the "internal hom" out of $X$) or completeness/cocompleteness. We refrain from doing so unless necessary.

\begin{definition}
    The virtual double category $\cat{V}-\bb{Cat}$ of $\cat{V}$-enriched categories has
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{objects:} $\cat{V}$-enriched categories.
        \item \textbf{arrows:} $\cat{V}$-functors.
        \item \textbf{distributors:} $\cat{V}$-distributors. A $\cat{V}$-distributor $p : X \dfrom Y$ comprises of an object $p(x, y)$ for all objects $x$ of $X$ and $y$ of $Y$, natural contravariantely in $x$ and covariantely in $y$ and compatible with composition and identities.
        \item \textbf{cells:} $\cat{V}$-natural transformations. A cell
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                C_0 \ar[d, "f"'] & \ar[l, loose, "p_1"'] \cdots & \ar[l, loose, "p_n"'] C_n \ar[d, "g"]\\
                D && \ar[ll, loose, "q"] D'
            \end{tikzcd}\]
            Is the data of a $\cat{V}$-natural morphism
            $$p_1(x_0, x_1) \otimes \cdots \otimes p_n(x_{n-1}, x_n) \to q(fx_0, gx_n)$$
            With a few commutative diagrams that can be found in \cite[Definition 8.1]{arkor2024formaltheoryrelativemonads}.
    \end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    This virtual double category is a virtual equipment.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    The loose identity on $A$ is given by $(x, y) \mapsto A(x, y)$ and the restriction by $p(f, g)(x, y) = p(fx, gy)$. The associated cell is the identity.
\end{proof}

Moreover, lax monoidal functors between base of enrichment are often called "change of base" and induce morphisms of virtual equipments defined as follows: Given a lax monoidal functor $F : (\cat V_1, \otimes_1, I_1) \to (\cat V_2, \otimes_2, I_2)$

\begin{itemize}
    \item It sends an $\cat V_1$-enriched category $C$ to the $\cat V_2$-enriched category $F(C)$ with the same objects and having hom-objects $F(C)(x, y) := F(C(x, y)$. Composition and units in $F(C)$ are the images of those in $C$.
    \item It sends a $\cat V_1$-functor $f$ to the $\cat V_2$-functor $F(f)$ given on objects by $f$ and on hom-objects by $F\circ f$
    \item It sends a $\cat V_1$-natural transformation $\sigma : f \To g$ to a $\cat V_2$-natural transformation $F(\sigma)$ given by applying $F$ to each component
    \item It sends a $\cat V_1$-distributor $p : A \dfrom B$ to a $\cat V_2$-distributor $F(p) : F(A) \dfrom F(B)$ given by post-composition by $F$
\end{itemize}

Recalling Proposition \ref{prop:rift_psh}, it is possible to describes the right lifts that exist:

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 8.5]{arkor2024formaltheoryrelativemonads}}]
    A $\cat{V}$-presheaf on a $\cat{V}$-category $C$ is the data of an object $p(c)$ of $\cat{V}$ for each object $c$ of $C$ compatible with "morphisms" of $C$ : $C(d, c) \otimes p(c) \to p(d)$ for all $c, d$ in $C$ compatible with the identity and composition in the evident way.

    For $p, q$ $\cat{V}$-presheaves on $C$ and $v$ an object of $C$, a family of morphisms $\set{\phi_c : p(c)\otimes v \to q(c)}_{c\in\ob(C)}$ is $\cat{V}$-natural in $c$ when for all $c'$, the following diagram commutes
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        C(c', c)\otimes p(c)\otimes v \dar \rar & C(c', c)\otimes q(c) \dar\\
        p(c')\otimes v \rar & q(c')
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    If there exists an object $\ca{P}C(p, q)$ of $\cat{V}$ with a universal $\cat{V}$-natural family, we call $\ca{P}C(p, q)$ the object of $\cat{V}$-natural transformations from $p$ to $q$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    When $\cat{V}$ is symmetric and closed, a $\cat{V}$-presheaf is just a $\cat{V}$-functor $C^\op \to \cat{V}$ and in that case, $\ca{P}C(p, q) \simeqq [C^\op, \cat{V}](p, q)$ (when it exists).
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[{\cite[Lemma 8.7]{arkor2024formaltheoryrelativemonads}}]
    Let $p : Z \dfrom Y, q : Y \dfrom X$ be $\cat{V}$-distributors. If the objects $\ca{P}C(p(-, x), q(-, y))$ for all $x\in\ob(X), y\in\ob(Y)$, they form the right lift $q\rift p : Y \dfrom X$.
\end{lemma}

Relative monads in enriched categories have an equivalent definition, which is easier to work with.

\begin{theorem}[{\cite[Theorem 8.12]{arkor2024formaltheoryrelativemonads}}]
    A relative monad in $\cat V-\bb{Cat}$ is equivalently specified by
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item A $\cat V$-functor $j : A \to E$
        \item A function on objects $t : \ob A \to \ob E$
        \item For each $x, y\in\ob A$, a morphism $\dag_{x,y} : E(jx, ty) \to E(tx, ty)$ in $\cat V$
        \item For each $x\in\ob A$, a morphism $\eta_x : I \to E(jx, ty)$
    \end{enumerate}
    satisfying the equations
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        E(jx, ty) \ar[dr, equal] \ar[r, "\dag_{x, y}"] & E(tx, ty) \ar[d, "{E(\eta_x, ty)}"]\\
                                                       & E(jx, ty)
    \end{tikzcd}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{tikzcd}
        I \ar[dr, "i_{tx}"] \ar[r, "\eta_x"] & E(jx, tx) \ar[d, "\dag_{x,x}"]\\
                                               & E(tx, tx)
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d, "\dag_{x,y}\otimes\dag_{y,z}"'] E(jx, ty)\otimes E(jy, tz) \ar[rr, "{E(jx, ty)\otimes\dag_{y, z}}"] && E(jx, ty)\otimes E(ty, tz) \ar[d, "\circ_{jx, ty, tz}"]\\
        E(tx, ty)\otimes E(ty, tz) \ar[dr, "\circ_{tx, ty, tz}"] && E(jx, ty) \ar[dl, "\dag_{x, y}"]\\
                                                                 & E(tx, tz)
    \end{tikzcd}\]
\end{theorem}

Interestingly enough, opalgebra objects always exist in $\cat V-\bb{Cat}$ and require no condition on $\cat V$:

\begin{theorem}[{\cite[Theorem 8.21]{arkor2024formaltheoryrelativemonads}}]
    Every relative monad in $\cat V-\bb{Cat}$ admits an opalgebra object.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
    Let $h : A \to E$ be a $\cat V$-functor and $T = (t, \dag, \eta)$ be a $j$-monad. The opalgebra object is given by the Kleisli $\cat V$-category of $T$ $\cat{Kl}(T)$, defined as follows:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $\ob\cat{Kl}(T) := \ob A$
        \item $\cat{Kl}(T)(x, y) := E(jx, ty)$
        \item $i_x := \eta_x$
        \item $\circ_{x, y, z} := (\circ_{jx, ty, tz}) \circ (E(jx, ty)\otimes\dag_{y, z})$
    \end{itemize}
    The arrow $k_T : A \to \cat{Kl}(T)$ is the identity on objects and is given on hom-objects by $(k_T)_{x, y} := E(jx, \eta_y)\circ j_{x, y}$.
\end{proof}

However, the existence of algebra objects is more complicated and requires more structure on $\cat V$. Constructing algebras is possible, the issue is assembling the algebras into an algebra object:

\begin{definition}
    Let $j : A \to E$ be a $\cat V$-functor and $T = (t, \dag, \eta)$ be a $j$-monad. An \textbf{Eilenberg-Moore $T$-algebra} comprises of
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item An object $e\in\ob E$
        \item A family of morphisms
            \[
                \set{\rtimes_x:E(jx, e)\to E(tx, e)}_{x\in\ob A}
            \]
    \end{enumerate}
    Such that the following diagrams commute for all $x, y\in\ob A$.
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        E(jx, e) \ar[dr, equal] \ar[r, "\rtimes_x"] & E(tx, e) \ar[d, "{E(\eta_x, e)}"]\\
                                                    & E(jx, e)
    \end{tikzcd}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d, "\dag_{x, y}\otimes\rtimes_y"'] E(jx, ty) \otimes E(jy, e) \ar[rr, "{E(jx, ty)\otimes\rtimes_y}"] && E(jx, ty) \otimes E(ty, e) \ar[d, "\circ_{jx, ty, e}"]\\
        \ar[dr, "\circ_{tx, ty, e}"'] E(tx, ty)\otimes E(ty, e) && E(jx, e) \ar[dl, "\rtimes_x"]\\
                                                                & E(tx, e)
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    Given two Eilenberg-Moore $T$-algebras $(e, \rtimes), (e', \rtimes')$ and $v$ an object of $\cat V$, a \textbf{$v$-graded morphism from $(e, \rtimes)$ to $(e', \rtimes')$} is a morphism $h : v \to E(e, e')$ such that the diagram commutes for all $x\in\ob A$
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d, "{E(jx, e)\otimes h}"'] E(jx, e)\otimes v \ar[r, "\rtimes_x\otimes v"] & E(tx, e)\otimes v) \ar[d, "{E(tx, e)\otimes h}"]\\
        \ar[d, "\circ_{jx, e, e'}"'] E(jx, e)\otimes E(e, e') & E(tx, e) \otimes E(e, e') \ar[d, "\circ_{tx, e, e'}"]\\
        E(jx, e') \ar[r, "\rtimes_x'"'] & E(tx, e')
    \end{tikzcd}\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[{\cite[Theorem 8.19]{arkor2024formaltheoryrelativemonads}}]
    Let $j : A \to E$ be a $\cat V$-functor and $T$ be a $j$-monad. $T$ admits an algebra object exactly when for all Eilenberg-Moore $T$-algebras $(e, \rtimes)$ and $(e', \rtimes')$ there is a graded morphism
    \[
        (u_T)_{(e, \rtimes), (e', \rtimes')} : \bb{EM}(T)((e, \rtimes), (e', \rtimes')) \to E(e, e')
    \]
    such that every graded morphism $v\to E(e, e')$ factors uniquely through $(u_T)_{(e, \rtimes), (e', \rtimes')}$ as a morphism $v \to \bb{EM}(T)((e, \rtimes), (e', \rtimes')) \to E(e, e')$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}
    If $\cat V$ is closed and admits equalizers, then every $j$-monad admits an algebra object.
\end{corollary}

The corollary above is a simplified version of \cite[Corollary 8.20]{arkor2024formaltheoryrelativemonads}. The closeness assumption is useless when considering non-relative monads.

\subsection{Internal and locally internal categories}
\label{subsec:internal}

We refer to \cite[Chapter 8]{borceux1} and \cite[Chapter 2]{johnstone} for an elementary treatment, and to \cite{betti} for a formal treatment. Overall, this subsection is heavily influenced by the work of Renato Betti, especially \cite{bettiwalters_closedbicats}, \cite{bettiwalters_completness}, \cite{betti}. We first use the characterization of internal categories as internal monads in the bicategories of spans.

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 5.3.1]{leinster}}]
    Let $\mathbb{X}$ be a virtual double category. We construct the virtual double category of horizontal monads $\Mod(\bb{X})$:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textit{Objects:} $(X_0, X : X_0 \dfrom X_0, \mu_X : X, X \To X, \eta_X : \To X)$ such that $\mu(\mu, 1_X) = \mu(1_X, \mu)$ et $\mu(\eta, 1_X) = \mu(1_X, \eta) = 1_X$. We will usually just write $X$ for $(X_0, X, \mu_X, \eta_X)$.
        \item \textit{Arrows:} $f := (f_0 : X_0 \to Y_0, \omega_f) : X \to Y$ such that $\omega_f \circ \mu_X = \mu_Y(\omega_f, \omega_f)$ et $\omega_f \circ \eta_X = \omega_Y\circ f_0$.
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                \ar[d, "f_0"'] X_0 & \ar[l, loose, "X"', ""{anchor=center,name=t}] X_0 \ar[d, "f_0"]\\
                Y_0 & \ar[l, loose, "Y", ""{anchor=center,name=b}] Y_0
                \ar[from=t, to=b, phantom, "\omega_f"{anchor=center}]
            \end{tikzcd}\]
        \item \textit{Distributors:} $p := (p : X_0 \dfrom Y_0, \chi, \varphi) : X \dfrom Y$ such that $\chi(1_p, \mu_Y) = \chi(\chi, 1_Y)$, $\chi(1_p, \eta_Y) = 1_p$ and dually $\varphi(\mu_X, 1_p) = \varphi(1_X, \varphi)$, $\varphi(\eta_X, 1_p) = 1_p$; et $\varphi(1_X, \xi) = \xi(\varphi, 1_Y)$.
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                \ar[d, equal] X_0 & \ar[l, loose, "p"'] Y_0 & \ar[l, loose, "Y"'] Y_0 \ar[d, equal]\\
                X_0 & {} & \ar[ll, loose, "p"] Y_0
                \ar[from=1-2, to=2-2, phantom, "\chi"{anchor=center}]
            \end{tikzcd}
            \hspace{10pt}
            \begin{tikzcd}
                    \ar[d, equal] X_0 & \ar[l, loose, "X"'] X_0 & \ar[l, loose, "p"'] Y_0 \ar[d, equal]\\
                    X_0 & {} & \ar[ll, loose, "p"] Y_0
                    \ar[from=1-2, to=2-2, phantom, "\varphi"{anchor=center}]
            \end{tikzcd}\]
        \item \textit{Cell:} A cell in $\Mod(\bb{X})$ is given by one in $\bb{X}$ with frame
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                \ar[d, "f_0"'] X_0 & \ar[l, loose, "p_1"'] \cdots & \ar[l, loose, "p_n"'] Y_0 \ar[d, "g_0"]\\
                X_0' & {} & \ar[ll, loose, "q"] Y_0'
                \ar[from=1-2, to=2-2, phantom, "\theta"{anchor=center}]
            \end{tikzcd}\]
            Such that $\theta(\varphi, 1\cdots 1) = \chi_q(\omega_f, \theta)$, $\theta(1\cdots 1, \chi) = \varphi_q(\theta, \omega_g)$ and for all $2 \leq i \leq n$, $\theta(1\cdots 1, \chi_{i-1}, 1\cdots 1) = \theta(1\cdots 1, \varphi_i, 1\cdots 1)$.
    \end{itemize}
\end{definition}

$\bb{X}$ having restrictions is a sufficient condition for $\Mod(\bb{X})$ to be a virtual equipment.

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition 5.5]{cs10}}]
    $\Mod(\bb{X})$ is normal and $A(1, 1)$ is given by $A$. That is, $\eta_A : \To A$ is opcartesian in $\Mod(\bb{X})$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition 7.4]{cs10}}]
    If $\bb{X}$ has all restrictions, then $\Mod(\bb{X})$ too, and the component of $p(f, g)$ is $p(f_0, g_0)$.
\end{proposition}

Returning to internal categories, fix $\cat{E}$ a category with pullbacks. We define $\Dist(\cat{E}) := \Mod(\Span(\cat{E}))$ and unravel the definitions:
\begin{itemize}
    \item An object is a monad in $\Span(\cat{E})$, or equivalently in the bicategory $\bSpan(\cat{E})$, that is, an \textbf{category internal to $\cat{E}$}.
    \item An arrow is a morphism preserving the monad structure, so an \textbf{internal functor}.
    \item A distributor $C \dfrom D$ is a diagram on $C^\op\times D$, so an \textbf{internal distributor}.
    \item A cell is an \textbf{internal natural transformation}.
\end{itemize}
 
Note that, when $\cat{E}$ has pullback stable reflexive coequalizers, we can compose horizontal arrows.

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition 4.1]{day_loccartclosed}}]
    When $\cat{E}$ has finite limits, $\bSpan\cat{E}$ has all right lifts and extensions (along/through unary weights) if and only if it is locally cartesian closed.
\end{proposition}

In particular, when $\cat{E}$ is an elementary topos, we can compose distributors and all rights and extensions exist.

\begin{remark}
    Note also that in the general case, since $\bSpan(\cat{E})$ is a symmetric bicategory, the existence of right extensions is equivalent to the existence of right liftings.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
    An internal category $C$ associated to a monad $\alpha$ is
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{discrete} if $\alpha \simeqq 1_{C_0}$.
        \item a \textbf{monoid} if $C_0$ is a terminal object.
        \item a \textbf{groupoid} if $\alpha$ is idempotent.
        \item a \textbf{group} if it is both a monoid and a groupoid.
    \end{itemize}
    Every object of $\bSpan(\cat{E})$, regarded with the trivial monad, is a \textbf{discrete internal category}. Switching the source and target arrows, we get the \textbf{opposite internal category} of $C$.
\end{definition}

Starting with something easy, for $f : C \to D$ an internal functor, the cell $\smallfrown_f$ looks like

\[\begin{tikzcd}
    C_0 \ar[d, equal] & \ar[l, "s"'] C_1 \ar[d, "{(s, f_1, t)}"] \ar[r, "t"] & C_0 \ar[d, equal]\\
    C_0 & \ar[l, "\pi_0"] C_0 \times_{D_0} D_1 \times_{D_0} C_0 \ar[r, "\pi_1"] & C_0
\end{tikzcd}\]

\begin{lemma}
    $f : C \to D$ is fully faithful if and only if $C_1$ is the iterated pullback
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \dar (C_0 \underset{D_0}{\times} D_1) \underset{D_1}{\times} (D_1 \underset{D_0}{\times} C_0) \rar \ar[dr, pullback] & \dar D_1 \underset{D_0}{\times} C_0 \rar \ar[dr, pullback] & C_0 \ar[d, "f_0"]\\
        \dar C_0 \underset{D_0}{\times} D_1 \rar \ar[dr, pullback] & \ar[d, "s"] D_1 \ar[r, "t"'] & D_0\\
        C_0 \ar[r, "f_0"'] & D_0
    \end{tikzcd}\]
\end{lemma}

To study internal categories, it is useful to introduce the notion of locally internal categories. We follow \cite{bettiwalters_closedbicats} and start with categories enriched in bicategories:

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 1.2]{bettiwalters_closedbicats}}]
    \index{category!enriched in a bicategory}\index{$\ca B$-category}
    For $\ca{B}$ a bicategory, a \textbf{$\ca{B}$-enriched category} $X$ comprises of the following data:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Objects $x, y, \cdots$
        \item For each object $x$, an underlying object $ex$ in $\ca B$.  We say that $x$ is an object over $ex$.
        \item For each pair $x, y$ of objects, an arrow $X(x, y) : ex \from ey$ in $\ca B$.
        \item For each object, a unit $1_{ex} \to X(x, x)$.
        \item A composition given by a cell $X(x, y) \cdot X(y, z) \to X(x, z)$ in $\ca B$.
    \end{itemize}
    and such that this data satisfy associativity and identity properties. A \textbf{$\ca{B}$-functor} $F : X \to Y$ is a function on objects preserving the underlying objects and a family of 2-cells
    $$X(x, y) \to Y(Fx, Fy)$$
    Satisfying the axioms of functors.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    In the case of $\ca{B} := \bSpan(\cat{E})$, a $\ca{B}$-category with a single object is an internal category: the underlying object is the object of objects, the Hom arrow gives the source and target morphisms, the unit gives the identity morphism and the composition is the internal composition.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 1.8]{bettiwalters_closedbicats}}]
    \label{def:intcat_res}
    For $X$ a $\ca B$-category, $x$ an object over $u$ and $f : v \to u$ a morphism in $\ca B$. A restriction $x_f$ of $x$ along $f$ is an object over $v$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        f \cdot X(x, y) &\simeqq X(x_f, y)\\
        X(y, x) \cdot f^\op &\simeqq X(y, x_f)
    \end{align*}
    $X$ has restrictions if for all $x$ and $f$, $x_f$ exists.
\end{definition}

We have two characterizations of a locally internal category.

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 1.11, Proposition 1.13 and Proposition 4.3]{bettiwalters_closedbicats}}]
    A \textbf{locally internal category} is a $\bSpan(\cat{E})$-category with restrictions. This is equivalent to a fibration
    $$p : \cat F \to \cat E$$
    Such that the functor $[x, y] : \bSpan(\cat E)(px, py)^\op \to \cat{Set}$ taking $(f, g)$ to $\cat F_w(f^*x, g^*y)$ (where $w$ is the domain of $f$ and $g$) is representable for all $x$ and $y$.
\end{definition}

Note that in the case of $\bSpan\cat E$, the two equations are equivalent. The definition of module between $\ca B$-categories will coincide with the one of internal categories when viewed as one object $\ca B$-categories.

\begin{definition}
    Let $X$ and $Y$ be $\ca B$-categories. A \textbf{$\ca B$-distributor} $p : X \dfrom Y$ consists of an arrow $p(x, y) : ex \from ey$ in $\ca B$ for each $x$ object in $X$ and $y$ object in $Y$, with actions of $X$ and $Y$ :
    \begin{align*}
        X(x', x) \cdot p(x, y) &\to p(x', y)\\
        p(x, y) \cdot Y(y, y') &\to p(x, y')
    \end{align*}
    With associativity, unity and mixed associativity properties.
\end{definition}

Using this definition, we can see the isomorphisms of Definition \ref{def:intcat_res} as isomorphisms of distributors, seeing an arrow in $\ca B$ as a distributors between discrete internal categories.

\begin{lemma}
    Functors between $\bSpan(\cat E)$-categories preserve restrictions.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Let $f : u \to v$ a map, $x$ an object over $v$ and $F : X \to Y$ a $\bSpan\cat E$-functor:
    $$Y(1, Fx_f) \simeqq Y(1, F) \cdot Y(1, x) \cdot f^\op \simeqq Y(1, (Fx)_f)$$
\end{proof}

As remarked in \cite{bettiwalters_completness}, when $\ca B$ is locally finitely complete and cocomplete, composites of the form $X \dfrom A \dfrom Y$ with $A$ having a finite number of objects (in particular, when $A$ is an internal category) always exist. Moreover, the composition of $p : X \dfrom Y$ and $q : Y \dfrom Z$, when it exists, is given by the coequalizer on the two actions in $\ca B(ex, ez)$:

\[\begin{tikzcd}
    \displaystyle{\sum_{y', y''}} p(x, y') \cdot Y(y', y'') \cdot q(y'', z) \ar[r, shift left] \ar[r, shift right]
    & \displaystyle{\sum_y} p(x, y) \cdot q(y, z)
\end{tikzcd}\]

When considering $\bSpan(\cat Set)$-categories (e.g. small categories), this is exactly the coend $\int^{y\in Y} q(y, z) \times p(x, y)$. 

The construction of presheaf objects of internal categories is relatively straightforward: For $A$ an internal category, $\PP$ a class of distributors, $\psh\PP A$ is constructed by taking as objects over $u$ the modules $A \dfrom u$ in $\PP$ and as hom the right extension $\psh\PP A(\alpha, \beta) = \beta \rext \alpha$. This is a category with restriction by \cite[Proposition 2.17]{bettiwalters_closedbicats}. For $B = \bSpan \cat E$, $\PP$ the class containing every distributor and $A = I$ the terminal internal category, $\psh\PP I$ can be thought as $\cat E$. Taking $\cat E = \cat{Set}$, the presheaf object of the terminal set is $\cat{Set}$.

\begin{remark}
    The definition of the homs agrees with \ref{prop:rift_psh} by definition.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
    Taking $B = \cat{Ab}$, an internal category is a ring and a module $R\dfrom S$ is a $(R, S)$-bimodule. Considering
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        C \ar[d, loose, "M\rext N"'] & \ar[l, loose, "N"'] A \ar[dl, loose, "M"]\\
        X
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    The right extension $M\rext N$ is therefore given by the $(C, X)$-bimodule of $A$-linear maps.
\end{example}

The Definition \ref{def:co_lim} of (co)limits coincide with \cite[Definition 3.1]{bettiwalters_closedbicats} and the article produces interesting examples, reproduced below:

\begin{example}
    Let $N$ be the sequence $\set{\frac{1}{n}}$ viewed as a category enriched over $\bb R^+$. A functor $N \to X$ is therefore a Cauchy sequence (or at least, every Cauchy sequence is equivalent to such a functor). Considering the module $\phi : I \dfrom N$, where $I$ is the terminal metric space, given by $\phi\left(\frac 1 n \right) = \frac 1 n$, we get
    \[\lim_{n\to+\infty}x_n = \lim{}^\phi x\]
\end{example}

\begin{example}
    Taking $B = \cat{Ab}$ and $R$ a ring, consider
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \bb Z \ar[rr, "M"] && R-\cat{Mod} \ar[dl, loose, "{\bb Z(1, M) \rift A}"]\\
                           & \ar[ul, loose, "A"] \bb Z
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    $M$ is an $R$-module, $A$ an abelian group and we have $R-\cat{Mod}(1, [A, M]) \simeqq \bb Z(1, M) \rift A$n therefore $[A, M]$ is the limit of $M$ weighted by $A$. Dually, $A\otimes_ZM$ can be seen as the $A$-weighted limit of $M$.
\end{example}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{$\infty$}{∞}-categories}
\label{subsec:inf_cats}

We resume here a few results from \cite{riehl_verity} using virtual equipments, establish a few limitations to the use of virtual equipments to study $\infty$-categories and mention alternatives frameworks. We assume that the reader is familiar with the theory of quasicategories, although we will not use much of the theory since we focus on a synthetic description. In the few proofs reproduced, most technical details are left out: the interested reader is referred to Riehl and Verity's book. It is also admitted that most constructions we want to do (slice and arrow $\infty$-cosmoï, simplicial (co)limits...), we can.

As mentioned in the Conventions (Subsection \ref{subsec:conventions}), our distributors go from right to left. This requires some changes when considering the formalism of two-sided fibrations, which means there might be some issues or errors with the following presentation, but also that those possible issues are without serious consequences since the whole section is more about the ideas than the technical details in the proofs.

The first natural place (or universe ?) where flavours of $\infty$-categories live is called an $\infty$-cosmos. Some technical definitions, in particular Leibniz cotensors, are omitted for brevity as they are not key to understand the use of virtual equipments in the study of flavours of $\infty$-categories.

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 1.2.1]{riehl_verity}}]
    \label{def:inf_cosmos}
    \index{\texorpdfstring{$\infty$}{∞}-cosmos}
    An \textbf{$\infty$-cosmos} $\ca K$ is a category enriched in quasicategories equipped with a collection of maps called \textbf{isofibrations} such that:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\ca K$ has a terminal object, small products, pullbacks of isofibrations, limits of countable towers of isofibrations and cotensors with simplicial sets; such that those limits are those when $\ca K$ is viewed as simplicially enriched.
        \item The class of isofibrations contains the isomorphisms, maps with codomain the terminal object, and is closed under composition, product, pullback, inverse limit of tower and Leibniz cotensor with monomorphisms. Moreover, we ask that for $f : A \to B$ an isofibration, the induced morphism $\Fun(X, A) \to \Fun(X, B)$ is an isofibration for any $X$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    Note that, in particular, an $\infty$-cosmos is a simplicially enriched category such that all Hom objects are weak Kan complexes. Hom objects of an $\infty$-cosmos are also called functor spaces. What we call "maps" are vertices of functor spaces.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
    \label{ex:infcats_cosmoi}
    Most models for $\infty$-categories form $\infty$-cosmoï:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Quasicategories define an $\infty$-cosmos in which the isofibrations are the usual isofibrations \cite[Proposition 1.2.10]{riehl_verity}.
        \item 1-categories and Kan complexes also form $\infty$-cosmoï \cite[Propositions 1.2.11 and 1.2.12]{riehl_verity}.
        \item Complete Segal spaces, Segal categories and 1-complicial sets define $\infty$-cosmoï \cite[Example 1.2.24]{riehl_verity}.
    \end{itemize}
\end{example}

An easy method to reduce this higher data to 2 dimensions is to take the homotopy 2-category:

\begin{definition}
    \index{\texorpdfstring{$\infty$}{∞}-cosmos!homotopy 2-category}
    Let $\ca K$ be an $\infty$-cosmos. Its \textbf{homotopy 2-category} is the 2-category $\fk h \ca K$ such that:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item The objects are the objects of $\ca K$.
        \item The 1-morphisms are the 0-arrows in the functor spaces.
        \item The 2-morphisms are homotopy classes of simplices in functor spaces.
    \end{itemize}
    i.e. the categories of morphisms are the homotopy categories of the (quasicategories) functor spaces
\end{definition}

Finally, there's a notion of morphism of $\infty$-cosmoï.

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Deinition 1.3.1]{riehl_verity}}]
    \label{def:cosmological_functor}
    \index{\texorpdfstring{$\infty$}{∞}!cosmological functor} \index{\texorpdfstring{$\infty$}{∞}!cosmological biequivalence}
    A \textbf{cosmological functor} is a simplicial functor between $\infty$-cosmoï preserving the isofibrations and all the cosmological limits. Given a cosmological functor $F : \ca K \to \ca L$, it is a \textbf{cosmological biequivalence} if
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item it is essentially surjective on objects up to equivalence: For all object $C$ of $\ca L$, there exists an object $A$ of $\ca K$ such that $FA \simeq C$.
        \item it defines a local equivalence: For all objects $A, B$ of $\ca K$, the action of $F$ defines an equivalence of quasicategories $\Fun(A, B) \xrightarrow{\sim} \Fun(FA, FB)$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    \label{ex:cosmological_biequiv}
    There are "underlying quasicategory" biequivalences from complete Segal spaces, Segal categories and 1-complicial sets to quasicategories, but also "nerve" biequivalences from quasicategories to complete Segal spaces and Segal categories and "discretization" from complete Segal spaces to Segal categories.
\end{example}

To construct a virtual double category, we still need horizontal arrows. For that, we use the class of isofibrations. A first approximation is two-sided fibrations, which will induce a double category:

\begin{definition}
    \index{two-sided fibrations}
    A \textbf{two-sided fibration} is a span $A \from E \to B$ such that the induced map $E \to A\times B$ is an isofibration.
\end{definition}

Two-sided isofibrations from $A$ to $B$ form an $\infty$-cosmos $\ca K_{/A\times B}$. Because we want our horizontal arrows to "act like Hom spaces", a natural condition is to ask for (co)cartesianity on each side.

We didn't introduce the notion of (co)cartesian fibration in an $\infty$-cosmos \cite[Definition 5.2.1]{riehl_verity}, and choose to think of them as the usual notion of Grothendieck fibrations. The reason for this is that, by working in a formal setting (in an $\infty$-cosmos), we have to define the cartesian fibration model-independently and in particular, "cartesian arrows". This is the subject of \cite[Section 5]{riehl_verity}, and we refer the interested (or confused) reader to the source. We admit that (co)cartesian fibrations form $\infty$-cosmoï $\Cart(\ca K)$ and $\coCart(\ca K)$ \cite[Section 6]{riehl_verity}. Note that $\Cart(\ca K)_{/B}$ (resp. $\coCart(\ca K)_{/B}$) refer not to the slice $\infty$-cosmos but to the $\infty$-cosmos of (co)cartesian fibrations over $B$.

\begin{definition}
    \index{two-sided isofibration!cartesian on the left}\index{two-sided isofibration!cocartesian on the right}
    A two-sided isofibration $A \xleftarrow{p} E \xrightarrow{q} B$ is \textbf{cartesian on the left} when
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[dr, "q"'] E \ar[rr, "{(q, p)}"] && A\times B \ar[dl, "\pi"]\\
                            & B
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    is a cartesian fibration in $\ca K_{/B}$. Dually, it is \textbf{cocartesian on the right} when
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[dr, "p"'] E \ar[rr, "{(q, p)}"] && A\times B \ar[dl, "\pi"]\\
                            & A
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    is a cocartesian fibration in $\ca K_{/A}$.
\end{definition}

When we want to consider both cartesianity on the left and cocartesianity on the right, we get a useful criterion:

\begin{lemma}[{\cite[Corollary 7.1.3]{riehl_verity}}]
    A two-sided isofibration $A \xleftarrow{p} E \xrightarrow{q} B$ is cocartesian on the right and cartesian on the left if either of the following equivalent conditions is satisfied:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The map
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                \ar[dr, "p"'] E \ar[rr, "{(q, p)}"] && A\times B \ar[dl, "\pi"]\\
                                                    & A
            \end{tikzcd}\]
            lies in $\Cart(\ca K)_{/A}$ and defines a cocartesian fibration in $\ca K_{/A}$.
        \item The map
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                \ar[dr, "q"'] E \ar[rr, "{(q, p)}"] && A\times B \ar[dl, "\pi"]\\
                                                    & B
            \end{tikzcd}\]
            lies in $\coCart(\ca K)_{/B}$ and defines a cartesian fibration in $\ca K_{/B}$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

To get closer to an object "acting like a distributor", we define two-sided fibrations:

\begin{definition}
    \index{two-sided fibration}
    A \textbf{two-sided fibration} $A \xleftarrow{p} E \xrightarrow{q} B$ is a two-sided isofibration such that either of the following equivalent conditions is satisfied:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The map
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                \ar[dr, "p"'] E \ar[rr, "{(q, p)}"] && A\times B \ar[dl, "\pi"]\\
                                                    & A
            \end{tikzcd}\]
            defines a cocartesian fibration in $\Cart(\ca K)_{/A}$.
        \item The map
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                \ar[dr, "q"'] E \ar[rr, "{(q, p)}"] && A\times B \ar[dl, "\pi"]\\
                                                    & B
            \end{tikzcd}\]
            defines a cartesian fibration in $\coCart(\ca K)_{/A}$
        \item $A \xleftarrow{p} E \xrightarrow{q} B$ is cartesian on the left, cocartesian on the right and given any pair of natural transformations as on the left, and a $q$-cocartesian lift $\psi : e \To \alpha_*e$ of $\alpha$ over $B$ a $p$-cartesian lift $\chi : \beta^*e\To e$ of $\beta$ over $A$ as on the right:
            \[\begin{tikzcd}[sep=large]
                & \ar[dl, "a"', ""{anchor=center,name=a}] X \ar[d, "e"{description}, ""{anchor=center,name=e}] \ar[dr, "b", ""{anchor=center,name=b,inner sep=0}] &\\
                A & \ar[l, "p"] E \ar[r, "q"'] & B
                \ar[from=e, to=a, Rightarrow, shorten=2mm, "\alpha"] \ar[from=b, to=e, Rightarrow, shorten=2mm, "\beta"]
                \end{tikzcd}
                \hspace{3em}
                \begin{tikzcd}[sep=large]
                & \ar[d, bend right=90, dashed, "\alpha_*e"', end anchor=north west, ""{anchor=west,name=ae}] X \ar[d, "d"{description}, ""{anchor=center,name=m}] \ar[d, bend left=90, dashed, "\beta^*e", end anchor=north east, ""{anchor=east,name=be}] &\\
                A & \ar[l, "p"] E \ar[r, "q"'] & B
                \ar[from=m, to=ae, Rightarrow, "\psi"] \ar[from=be, to=m, Rightarrow, "\chi"]
                \end{tikzcd}
            \]
            There is a unique isomorphism $\alpha_*\beta^*e\simeqq\beta^*\alpha_*e$ over $A\times B$ commuting with the $p$-cartesian lift of $p\psi\cdot\beta$ over $A$ and the $p$-cocartesian lift of $\alpha\cdot q\chi$ over $B$.
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                & \ar[dl, Rightarrow, "\psi"'] e &\\
                \alpha_*e && \ar[ul, Rightarrow, "\chi"'] \beta^*e \ar[dl, Rightarrow, "\psi_{\alpha\cdot q\chi}", end anchor=north east]\\
                          & \ar[ul, Rightarrow, "\chi_{p\psi\cdot\beta}", start anchor=north west] \beta^*\alpha_*e\simeqq\beta^*\alpha_*e
            \end{tikzcd}\]
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    The last condition can be understood as "the cartesian and cocartesian properties commute" i.e. the processes of taking $p$-cartesian lift and $q$-cocartesian lift commute.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[{\cite[Proposition 7.1.6]{riehl_verity}}]
    For any object $A$ in an $\infty$-cosmos, the two-sided isofibration $A \xleftarrow{p_0} A^{\bb n} \xrightarrow{p_{n-1}} A$ is a two-sided fibration. In particular, $A \xleftarrow{p_0} A^{\bb 2} \xrightarrow{p_1} A$ is a two-sided fibration. Note that we use the property that $\infty$-cosmoï have cotensors by simplicial sets.
\end{example}

Finally, we can define what horizontal arrows will be:

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 7.4.1]{riehl_verity}}]
    \index{\texorpdfstring{$\infty$}{∞}-cosmos!module}
    A \textbf{module} from $A$ to $B$ in a $\infty$-cosmos is a two sided fibration $A \from E \to B$ that is a discrete object in $\prescript{}{A\backslash}\Fib(\ca K)_{/B}$ (i.e. the functor spaces with codomain the module are Kan complexes).
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[{\cite[Proposition 7.4.6]{riehl_verity}}]
    Comma object in an $\infty$-cosmos are modules and so are arrow objects (cotensor by $\bb 2$).
\end{example}

Lastly, we need morphisms of modules:

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 8.1.4]{riehl_verity}}]
    \index{\texorpdfstring{$\infty$}{∞}-cosmos!morphism of module}
    A \textbf{morphism of modules} from $A \from E \to B$ to $X \from F \to Y$
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d, "f"'] A & \ar[l, loose, "E"', ""{anchor=center,name=t}] B \ar[d, "g"]\\
        X & \ar[l, loose, "F", ""{anchor=center,name=b}] Y
        \ar[from=t, to=b, phantom, "\alpha"{anchor=center}]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    is a fibered isomorphism class of \textit{strictly} commuting functors $a$
    \[\begin{tikzcd}[sep=large]
        \ar[d, "f"'] A & \ar[l, "p'"'] E \ar[d, "a"{description}] \ar[r, "q'"] & B \ar[d, "g"]\\
        X & \ar[l, "p"] F \ar[r, "q"'] & Y
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    where two such functors $a$ and $a'$ are equivalent if there exists a natural isomorphism $\gamma : a\simeqq a'$ such that $q\gamma = \id_{aq'}$ and $p\gamma = \id_{bp'}$.
\end{definition}

We do not introduce fibered maps of two-sided isofibrations, as they are not relevant to our goal. It is worth mentioning that using two-sided isofibrations, we get a double category. However the composition (span composition) is flawed and the evident horizontal unit, the identity span, is useless. Constructing the virtual equipment associated to an $\infty$-cosmos, unit distributors will be the arrow categories (which will match the intuition given by the virtual equipments of 1-categories or enriched categories).

\begin{lemma}[{\cite[Lemma 8.1.11]{riehl_verity}}]
    For $\ca K$ an $\infty$-cosmos, there is a virtual double category of two-sided isofibrations whose objects and arrows are those of $\ca K$, whose horizontal arrows are two-sided isofibrations and whose $n$-ary cells are maps of two-sided isofibrations over $f\times g$
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d, "f"'] A_0 & \ar[l, loose, "E_1"'] \cdots & \ar[l, loose, "E_n"'] A_n \ar[d, "g"]\\
        B_0 &{}& \ar[ll, loose, "F"]
        \ar[from=1-2, to=2-2, phantom, "\alpha"{anchor=center}]
    \end{tikzcd}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{tikzcd}[sep=small]
        & \ar[dl, start anchor=south west] E_1 \times_{A_1}\cdots\times_{A_{n-1}}E_n \ar[ddd, dashed, "a"{description}] \ar[dr, start anchor=south east] &\\
        \ar[d, "f"'] A && A_n \ar[d, "g"]\\
        B_0 && B_n\\
            & \ar[ul] F \ar[ur]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{corollary}
    Restricting the class of horizontal arrows, we get the virtual double category $\bb Mod(\ca K)$ whose objects and arrows are those of $\ca K$, whose horizontal arrows are modules and whose $n$-ary cells are maps of modules over $f\times g$.
\end{corollary}

What's left is to show that this virtual double category is a virtual equipment, that is, it admits restrictions and horizontal units.

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition 8.2.1]{riehl_verity}}]
    $\bb Mod(\ca K)$ admits restriction.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
    Consider the following frame:
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d, "f"'] A & B \ar[d, "g"]\\
        X & \ar[l, loose, "E"] Y
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    The horizontal source of the cartesian cell is constructed as the pullback
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d] E(f, g) \ar[dr, pullback] \ar[r, "\rho"] & E \ar[d]\\
        A\times B \ar[r, "f\times g"'] & X \times Y
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    This defines a module from $B$ to $A$. The universal property of the cartesian cell is represented by the equivalence of functor spaces induced by the simplicial pullback:
    \[
        \Fun_{f\times g}(E_1\times_{A_1}\cdots\times_{A_{n-1}}E_n, E(f, g))\xrightarrow[\sim]{\rho\circ-}\Fun_{fa\times gb}(E_1\times_{A_1}\cdots\times_{A_{n-1}}E_n, E(f, g))
    \]
    Where $a$ and $b$ are as follow
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d, "fa"'] X_0 & \ar[l, loose, "E_1"'] \cdots & \ar[l, loose, "E_n"'] X_n \ar[d, "gb"]\\
        X &{} & \ar[ll, loose, "E"] Y
        \ar[from=1-2, to=2-2, phantom, "\phi"{anchor=center}]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    The equivalence descends to a bijection on isomorphism classes of objects, therefore we have a factorization
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d, "a"'] X_0 & \ar[l, loose, "E_1"'] \cdots & \ar[l, loose, "E_n"'] X_n \ar[d, "b"]\\
        \ar[d, "f"'] A &{} & \ar[ll, loose, "{E(f, g)}"] B \ar[d, "g"]\\
        X &{} & \ar[ll, loose, "E"] Y
        \ar[from=1-2, to=2-2, phantom, "\tilde\phi"{anchor=center}]
        \ar[from=2-2, to=3-2, phantom, "\rho"{anchor=center}]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition 8.2.4]{riehl_verity}}]
    $\bb Mod(\ca K)$ admits horizontal units.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
    The nullary cell is the following:
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        & \ar[dl, equal] A \ar[dd, "\iota"] \ar[dr, equal] &\\
        A && A\\
          & \ar[ul, "p_0"] A^{\bb 2} \ar[ur, "p_1"']
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    Where $\iota$ is given by the weak universal property of the arrow object \cite[Proposition 3.2.5]{riehl_verity} applied to the identity functor. For the universal property, there are two cases:
    \[\begin{tikzcd}
        \ar[d, "f"'] X & \ar[l, loose, "E_1"'] \cdots & \ar[l, loose, "E_n"'] A & \ar[l, loose, "F_1"'] \cdots & \ar[l, loose, "F_m"'] Y \ar[d, "g"]\\
        B &&{}&& \ar[llll, loose, "G"] C
        \ar[from=1-3, to=2-3, phantom, "\rho"{description}]
    \end{tikzcd}\]
    \begin{itemize}
        \item If $n = m = 0$, we conclude using the Yoneda Lemma for modules that the restriction gives a equivalence of functor spaces
            \[
                \Fun_{A\times A}(A^{\bb 2}, G(f, g)) \xrightarrow{\sim} \Fun_{A\times A}(A, G(f, g))
            \]
            Passing to isomorphism classes of objects, we get the universal property
        \item Otherwise, we take the horizontal composite of both sequences to get $X \xleftarrow{p} E \xrightarrow{q} A$ and $A \xleftarrow{r} E \xrightarrow{s} Y$. We have the following pullbacks:
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                &&& \ar[dl] E\times_A\Hom_A\times_AF \ar[dd, pullback] \ar[dr] &&&\\
                && \ar[dl] \Hom_A(q, A) \ar[dd, pullback] \ar[dr] &{}& \ar[dl] \Hom_A(A, s) \ar[dr] \ar[dd, pullback]\\
                & \ar[dl, "p"'] E \ar[dr, "q"] &{}& \ar[dl, "p_0"'] \Hom_A \ar[dr, "p_1"] &{}& \ar[dl, "r"'] F \ar[dr, "s"]\\
                X && A && A && Y
            \end{tikzcd}\]
            The $E$ horizontal composite is the two-sided fibration $(qp_0, p_1) : \Hom_A(q, A)$ and the $F$ one is $(p_0, rp_1)$. Pulling back the induced fibered adjunctions, we get:
            \[\begin{tikzcd}
                \ar[dr] E\times_AF \ar[rr, bend left=10, "E\times_A\iota\times_AF", ""{anchor=center,name=t}] && \ar[ll, bend left=10, dashed, "r", ""{anchor=center,name=b}] E\times_A\Hom_A\times_AF \ar[dl]\\
                & X\times Y
                \ar[from=t, to=b, adj]
            \end{tikzcd}\]
            Mapping into the discrete object $G(f, g)$ and restricting along $\iota$, we get an equivalence of Kan complexes
            \[
                \Fun_{X\times Y}(E\times_A\Hom_A\times_AF, G(f, g))\xrightarrow[\sim]{-\circ E\times_A\iota\times_AE} \Fun_{X\times Y}(E\times_AF, G(f, g))
            \]
            Taking isomorphism classes of objects, we get the universal property.
    \end{itemize}
\end{proof}

In conclusion, $\bb Mod(\ca K)$ is a virtual equipment. The various propositions in \cite[Sections 8.3 and 8.4]{riehl_verity}, with the exception of strong composites and cocartesian cells, are consequences of results due to \cite{cs10} in Section \ref{sec:fct}. For example, \cite[Corollary 8.4.6]{riehl_verity} is Theorem \ref{th:spider} and \cite[Proposition 8.4.7]{riehl_verity} is a consequence of Theorem \ref{th:comp_rest}.

The results in \cite[Sections 9.1, 9.4, 9.5]{riehl_verity} are generalized in Subsection \ref{subsec:ext_lift_colims}. The main appeal of virtual equipments for $\infty$-categories is studied in \cite[Chapters 10 and 11]{riehl_verity}: model independence.

Recall the many $\infty$-cosmoï of models of $(\infty, 1)$-categories introduced in Example \ref{ex:infcats_cosmoi}, related using the cosmological biequivalences mentioned in Example \ref{ex:cosmological_biequiv} :

\[\begin{tikzcd}
    \ca{CSS} \ar[rr, bend left=10, "\cat{disc}"{description}] \ar[dr, bend left, "{(-)_0}"{description}] && \ar[dl, bend right, "{(-)_0}"{description}] \ca{Segal}\\
    & \ar[ul, bend left, "\cat{nerve}"{description}] \ca{QCat} \ar[ur, bend right, "\cat{nerve}"{description}] \ar[d, bend left, "\natural"]\\
    & \ar[u, bend left, "{(-)_0}"] 1-\ca{Comp}
\end{tikzcd}\]

Cosmological functors, on their own, already preserve a lot of structure: equivalences, adjunctions, comma spans and cones, limits and colimits indexed by a simplicial set, modules and represented modules... \cite[Proposition 10.1.4]{riehl_verity}. Cosmological biequivalences therefore preserve a lot \textit{more} structure: given a cosmological biequivalence $F : \ca K \xrightarrow{\sim} \ca L$, for every object $B$ in $\ca K$, $F : \ca K_{/B} \xrightarrow{\sim} \ca L_{/FB}$ is also a cosmological biequivalence. Same for the induced functor on isofibrations $\infty$-cosmoï; and the induced homotopy functor $\fk hF : \fk h\ca K \to \fk h \ca L$ is a biequivalence. They are to be thought of as "change of model" functors: they not only preserve but also reflect all of the structure mentioned above, and more. From the very first observation of the paragraph we get the following result:

\begin{lemma}[{\cite[Lemma 11.1.1 and Proposition 11.1.2]{riehl_verity}}]
    Cosmological functors induce morphism of virtual double categories. This morphism extends to a morphism of virtual equipment, preserving restrictions and units.
\end{lemma}

Cosmological biequivalences also have the nice property of not only preserving and reflecting, but also creating modules: an isofibration is a module if and only if its image is a module, two modules are equivalent if and only if their respective images are... The first important result of this section is the model-independence of $\infty$-category theory:

\begin{theorem}[{\cite[Theorem 11.1.6]{riehl_verity}}]
    If $F : \ca K \xrightarrow{\sim} \ca L$ is a cosmological biequivalence, then the induced functor of virtual equipments
    \[
        F : \bb Mod(\ca K) \xrightarrow{\sim} \bb Mod(\ca L)
    \]
    defines an equivalence of virtual equipments.
\end{theorem}

An immediate corollary, by applying the theorem multiple times, gives a biequivalence for virtual equipments associated to $\infty$-cosmos related by a finite zig-zag of cosmological biequivalences. The idea behind the theorem is that a cosmological biequivalence transports theorem proven using the virtual equipments both ways i.e. the theories (or models) are equivalent from the point of view of virtual equipments. We shall not make this precise, as it requires introducing FOLDS and the idea of the result is understandable without the whole technical framework:

\begin{theorem}[{\cite[Corollary 11.3.10]{riehl_verity}}]
    Formulae in the language of virtual equipments are invariant under biequivalence of $\infty$-cosmoï.
\end{theorem}

This reduces a lot of proofs to formal ones in the appropriate virtual equipments, as is done in \cite[Section 9]{riehl_verity}. In particular, Kan extensions or (co)limits can be studied formally. However, as noted in \cite[Subsection 1.3]{riehl2015homotopycoherentadjunctionsformal}, although homotopy coherent monads induce monads in the 2-category, monads in the 2-category are not automatically homotopy coherent monads, as they require non trivial coherence (or operations) in each dimensions. In that case, virtual equipments are only partially useful...

We conclude this section by mentioning alternative frameworks: the first one, formal $\infty$-category theory as developed in \cite{ruit2025formalcategorytheoryinftyequipments} and using $\infty$-equipments, the $\infty$-categorical analogue of equipments; and the second one, synthetic category theory as developed in \cite{cisinski_synthetic} with an axiomatic treatment.
